People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

And when folks call about the repast at the center, I let it ring… I don’t answer.\n\nEven the music—second-line music, if it comes on, I turn it off right away. It just pulls me straight back there, and I… I can’t seem to bear it. Is that what you mean by avoiding?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I haven’t really thought of it that way. I still try to do my little things when I can, like keeping my hands busy. Some days I don’t feel much like it, but I don’t know if that’s the same as losing interest.\n\nIs that what you mean, doctor?\n\nDoctor: Are there things you usually enjoy doing that you've found no pleasure in since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so. I don’t want to sound ungrateful, because my daughter has been so good to me. She’ll sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know in my head that I love her… of course I do.\n\nBut since my grandson was killed, I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me. Not the way I should. It’s like the pleasure or comfort just doesn’t reach me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find very enjoyable before your grandson's death, but now you don't find enjoyable at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the biggest thing is being with my daughter. That used to bring me such comfort, just sitting with her, feeling her close.\n\nNow she can sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know I love her—I do—but I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. I’m sorry if that sounds awful. It’s not her fault. It’s just… the feeling doesn’t come.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt this lack of warmth or pleasure when spending time with your daughter?\n\nPatient: It’s been… since it happened, doctor. Since my grandson was killed outside the community center.\n\nSo, about six days now. I keep thinking maybe it will lift, and I don’t want to make too much of it, but it’s been there the whole time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F43.22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m sorry, I’m not sure how to say it the right way. Since my grandson was killed, I’ve been having this awful… numbness, I suppose. My daughter can sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know I love her—of course I do—but I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. That’s what frightened me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this numbness since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: It’s been… six days now. Since the day he was killed outside the community center. I know that isn’t very long, maybe I should be handling it better, but the numb feeling has been there the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're detached from yourself or the world around you?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. But sometimes my own living room looks sort of flat and strange, like I’m seeing it through church glass. And then I feel like I’m watching this old woman moving around the room, rather than being quite inside my own body. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any recurring memories or dreams about your grandson's death that feel disturbing or intrusive to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I don’t want to make too much of it, but it comes on me when I’m doing ordinary things. I’ll be folding a quilt square, or just reaching for my tea, and all of a sudden I hear those shots again… and I see him lying there outside the community center, like it’s right in front of me again. It’s very upsetting. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of your grandson?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, I suppose. I don’t mean to be difficult or rude to anyone, but I just can’t go near that street. And when folks call about the repast at the center, I let it ring… I don’t answer.\n\nEven the music—second-line music, if it comes on, I turn it off right away. It just pulls me straight back there, and I… I can’t seem to bear it. Is that what you mean by avoiding?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I haven’t really thought of it that way. I still try to do my little things when I can, like keeping my hands busy. Some days I don’t feel much like it, but I don’t know if that’s the same as losing interest.\n\nIs that what you mean, doctor?\n\nDoctor: Are there things you usually enjoy doing that you've found no pleasure in since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so. I don’t want to sound ungrateful, because my daughter has been so good to me. She’ll sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know in my head that I love her… of course I do.\n\nBut since my grandson was killed, I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me. Not the way I should. It’s like the pleasure or comfort just doesn’t reach me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find very enjoyable before your grandson's death, but now you don't find enjoyable at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the biggest thing is being with my daughter. That used to bring me such comfort, just sitting with her, feeling her close.\n\nNow she can sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know I love her—I do—but I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. I’m sorry if that sounds awful. It’s not her fault. It’s just… the feeling doesn’t come.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt this lack of warmth or pleasure when spending time with your daughter?\n\nPatient: It’s been… since it happened, doctor. Since my grandson was killed outside the community center.\n\nSo, about six days now. I keep thinking maybe it will lift, and I don’t want to make too much of it, but it’s been there the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have completely stopped doing since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes. I haven’t gone to my walking club at all, and I usually don’t miss that. They’re kind people, and it’s good for me, I know.\n\nAnd I’m ashamed to say I haven’t been able to help with the funeral repast planning either. My family depends on me for those sorts of things, and those are the very people I should be showing up for. I just… I can’t seem to bring myself to do it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I don’t mean to sound ungrateful, because my daughter has been so good to me, but that’s what frightens me a little.\n\nShe can sit right next to me and hold my hand, and I know I love her, of course I do… but I can’t feel that warmth inside the way I used to. It’s like the feeling is supposed to be there and it just… won’t come. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I… yes, I think so. The hardest thing to admit is that even with my daughter right there beside me, holding my hand, I know in my head that I love her, but I just can’t feel the warmth of it inside me.

Since the day he was killed outside the community center. I know that isn’t very long, maybe I should be handling it better, but the numb feeling has been there the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're detached from yourself or the world around you?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. But sometimes my own living room looks sort of flat and strange, like I’m seeing it through church glass. It just pulls me straight back there, and I… I can’t seem to bear it. Is that what you mean by avoiding?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I haven’t really thought of it that way. Yes… I’m afraid I have. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but since my grandson was killed, even the things that used to feel comforting don’t reach me the same way.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F43.22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m sorry, I’m not sure how to say it the right way. Since my grandson was killed, I’ve been having this awful… numbness, I suppose. My daughter can sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know I love her—of course I do—but I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. That’s what frightened me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this numbness since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: It’s been… six days now. Since the day he was killed outside the community center. I know that isn’t very long, maybe I should be handling it better, but the numb feeling has been there the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're detached from yourself or the world around you?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. But sometimes my own living room looks sort of flat and strange, like I’m seeing it through church glass. And then I feel like I’m watching this old woman moving around the room, rather than being quite inside my own body. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any recurring memories or dreams about your grandson's death that feel disturbing or intrusive to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I don’t want to make too much of it, but it comes on me when I’m doing ordinary things. I’ll be folding a quilt square, or just reaching for my tea, and all of a sudden I hear those shots again… and I see him lying there outside the community center, like it’s right in front of me again. It’s very upsetting. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of your grandson?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, I suppose. I don’t mean to be difficult or rude to anyone, but I just can’t go near that street. And when folks call about the repast at the center, I let it ring… I don’t answer.\n\nEven the music—second-line music, if it comes on, I turn it off right away. It just pulls me straight back there, and I… I can’t seem to bear it. Is that what you mean by avoiding?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I haven’t really thought of it that way. I still try to do my little things when I can, like keeping my hands busy. Some days I don’t feel much like it, but I don’t know if that’s the same as losing interest.\n\nIs that what you mean, doctor?\n\nDoctor: Are there things you usually enjoy doing that you've found no pleasure in since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so. I don’t want to sound ungrateful, because my daughter has been so good to me. She’ll sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know in my head that I love her… of course I do.\n\nBut since my grandson was killed, I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me. Not the way I should. It’s like the pleasure or comfort just doesn’t reach me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find very enjoyable before your grandson's death, but now you don't find enjoyable at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the biggest thing is being with my daughter. That used to bring me such comfort, just sitting with her, feeling her close.\n\nNow she can sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know I love her—I do—but I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. I’m sorry if that sounds awful. It’s not her fault. It’s just… the feeling doesn’t come.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt this lack of warmth or pleasure when spending time with your daughter?\n\nPatient: It’s been… since it happened, doctor. Since my grandson was killed outside the community center.\n\nSo, about six days now. I keep thinking maybe it will lift, and I don’t want to make too much of it, but it’s been there the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have completely stopped doing since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes. I haven’t gone to my walking club at all, and I usually don’t miss that. They’re kind people, and it’s good for me, I know.\n\nAnd I’m ashamed to say I haven’t been able to help with the funeral repast planning either. My family depends on me for those sorts of things, and those are the very people I should be showing up for. I just… I can’t seem to bring myself to do it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I don’t mean to sound ungrateful, because my daughter has been so good to me, but that’s what frightens me a little.\n\nShe can sit right next to me and hold my hand, and I know I love her, of course I do… but I can’t feel that warmth inside the way I used to. It’s like the feeling is supposed to be there and it just… won’t come. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there activities that you used to find very enjoyable before your grandson's death, but now find you have no interest in at all?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so. And now it’s like something in me has gone quiet. I’m sorry, I don’t know if I’m explaining it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you have no interest at all in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I have.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F43.22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m sorry, I’m not sure how to say it the right way. Since my grandson was killed, I’ve been having this awful… numbness, I suppose. My daughter can sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know I love her—of course I do—but I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. That’s what frightened me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this numbness since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: It’s been… six days now. Since the day he was killed outside the community center. I know that isn’t very long, maybe I should be handling it better, but the numb feeling has been there the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're detached from yourself or the world around you?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. But sometimes my own living room looks sort of flat and strange, like I’m seeing it through church glass. And then I feel like I’m watching this old woman moving around the room, rather than being quite inside my own body. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any recurring memories or dreams about your grandson's death that feel disturbing or intrusive to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I don’t want to make too much of it, but it comes on me when I’m doing ordinary things. I’ll be folding a quilt square, or just reaching for my tea, and all of a sudden I hear those shots again… and I see him lying there outside the community center, like it’s right in front of me again. It’s very upsetting. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of your grandson?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, I suppose. I don’t mean to be difficult or rude to anyone, but I just can’t go near that street. And when folks call about the repast at the center, I let it ring… I don’t answer.\n\nEven the music—second-line music, if it comes on, I turn it off right away. It just pulls me straight back there, and I… I can’t seem to bear it. Is that what you mean by avoiding?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I haven’t really thought of it that way. I still try to do my little things when I can, like keeping my hands busy. Some days I don’t feel much like it, but I don’t know if that’s the same as losing interest.\n\nIs that what you mean, doctor?\n\nDoctor: Are there things you usually enjoy doing that you've found no pleasure in since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so. I don’t want to sound ungrateful, because my daughter has been so good to me. She’ll sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know in my head that I love her… of course I do.\n\nBut since my grandson was killed, I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me. Not the way I should. It’s like the pleasure or comfort just doesn’t reach me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find very enjoyable before your grandson's death, but now you don't find enjoyable at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the biggest thing is being with my daughter. That used to bring me such comfort, just sitting with her, feeling her close.\n\nNow she can sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know I love her—I do—but I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. I’m sorry if that sounds awful. It’s not her fault. It’s just… the feeling doesn’t come.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt this lack of warmth or pleasure when spending time with your daughter?\n\nPatient: It’s been… since it happened, doctor. Since my grandson was killed outside the community center.\n\nSo, about six days now. I keep thinking maybe it will lift, and I don’t want to make too much of it, but it’s been there the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have completely stopped doing since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes. I haven’t gone to my walking club at all, and I usually don’t miss that. They’re kind people, and it’s good for me, I know.\n\nAnd I’m ashamed to say I haven’t been able to help with the funeral repast planning either. My family depends on me for those sorts of things, and those are the very people I should be showing up for. I just… I can’t seem to bring myself to do it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I don’t mean to sound ungrateful, because my daughter has been so good to me, but that’s what frightens me a little.\n\nShe can sit right next to me and hold my hand, and I know I love her, of course I do… but I can’t feel that warmth inside the way I used to. It’s like the feeling is supposed to be there and it just… won’t come. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there activities that you used to find very enjoyable before your grandson's death, but now find you have no interest in at all?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so. The hardest thing to admit is that even with my daughter right there beside me, holding my hand, I know in my head that I love her, but I just can’t feel the warmth of it inside me.\n\nThat used to be such a comfort to me, being with her. And now it’s like something in me has gone quiet. I’m sorry, I don’t know if I’m explaining it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you have no interest at all in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I have. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but since my grandson was killed, even the things that used to feel comforting don’t reach me the same way.\n\nMy daughter can be sitting right beside me, holding my hand, and I know I love her—I do know that—but I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. That’s probably the best way I can explain it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that you have less interest in almost all activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but it does feel like most things just don’t reach me anymore.\n\nI came in because since my grandson was killed, even when my daughter sits right beside me and holds my hand, I know I love her… but I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s a terrible thing to say, isn’t it? But that’s what it feels like.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I still try to do my little things when I can, like keeping my hands busy. Some days I don’t feel much like it, but I don’t know if that’s the same as losing interest.\n\nIs that what you mean, doctor?\n\nDoctor: Are there things you usually enjoy doing that you've found no pleasure in since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so. I don’t want to sound ungrateful, because my daughter has been so good to me. She’ll sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know in my head that I love her… of course I do.\n\nBut since my grandson was killed, I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me. Not the way I should. It’s like the pleasure or comfort just doesn’t reach me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find very enjoyable before your grandson's death, but now you don't find enjoyable at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the biggest thing is being with my daughter. That used to bring me such comfort, just sitting with her, feeling her close.\n\nNow she can sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know I love her—I do—but I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. I’m sorry if that sounds awful. It’s not her fault. It’s just… the feeling doesn’t come.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt this lack of warmth or pleasure when spending time with your daughter?\n\nPatient: It’s been… since it happened, doctor. Since my grandson was killed outside the community center.\n\nSo, about six days now. I keep thinking maybe it will lift, and I don’t want to make too much of it, but it’s been there the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have completely stopped doing since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes. I haven’t gone to my walking club at all, and I usually don’t miss that. They’re kind people, and it’s good for me, I know.\n\nAnd I’m ashamed to say I haven’t been able to help with the funeral repast planning either. My family depends on me for those sorts of things, and those are the very people I should be showing up for. I just… I can’t seem to bring myself to do it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I haven’t gone to my walking club at all, and I usually don’t miss that. I’m sorry if that sounds awful. It’s not her fault. Some days I don’t feel much like it, but I don’t know if that’s the same as losing interest.\n\nIs that what you mean, doctor?\n\nDoctor: Are there things you usually enjoy doing that you've found no pleasure in since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so. I don’t want to sound ungrateful, because my daughter has been so good to me. She’ll sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know in my head that I love her… of course I do.\n\nBut since my grandson was killed, I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me. Not the way I should. It’s like the pleasure or comfort just doesn’t reach me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find very enjoyable before your grandson's death, but now you don't find enjoyable at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the biggest thing is being with my daughter. That used to bring me such comfort, just sitting with her, feeling her close.\n\nNow she can sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know I love her—I do—but I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. Since my grandson was killed outside the community center.\n\nSo, about six days now. I keep thinking maybe it will lift, and I don’t want to make too much of it, but it’s been there the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have completely stopped doing since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes. I haven’t gone to my walking club at all, and I usually don’t miss that.
